TUESDAY.SOVmBm^l^ �Clinton focuses on blue-collar men in rally for GATT By Paul Bedard and David R. Sands Special section THE WASHINQTON TIMES A special section on the General Agreement on Tariffs and U-ade treaty: • One thing's for sure, there's no general agreement. E l • TTade issues sometimes get lost in GATT politics. E l • The rise of the World U-ade Organization. E2 • Interest groups on WTO. E3 • Commentary. E4, E6 Desperate for a postelection pick-me-up, President Clinton yesterday staged a bipartisan White House lovefest in support of the global GATT trade deal. The House of Representatives is expected to approve the accord by a comfortable margin in a vote today, but the contest is expected to be much closer in the Senate, which votes Thursday. Flanked by his Cabinet, prominent Republicans, Nobel laureates, and portraits of George and Martha Washington, Mr. Clinton issued a targeted appeal to the class of Americans who dislike him the most — non-college-educated male workers. The free-trade accord, signed by more than 120 nations in April, would provide American families with a $1,700 income bonus and help "solve the underlying anxiety" of blue-collar workers, whose real incomes have shrunk over the past IS years, Mr. Clinton argued. Tbp administration aides predicted GATT would pass but added see CLINTON, page A13 Photo by TfaeyA. WoodvwntThe Washington Times House Speaker Thomas Foley (left) and Rep. Robert Michel talk during a GATT rally at the White House. CLINTON From page Al they were not overconfident. U.S. TVade Representative Mickey Kantor refused to reveal the administration's private vote counts in the Senate, where 60 votes will be needed to clear a procedural hurdle relating to GATT's effect on the federal budget. "We hope we are going to get there," said Rufus Yerxa, Mr. Kantor's top deputy. Capitol Hill sources conceded that the administration still hasn't nailed down the votes it needs in the Senate, even with last week's much-touted endorsement by Senate Minority Leader Bob Dole, Kansas Republican. GATT foes gained a convert yesterday when Sen. Hank Brown, Colorado Republican, announced he would vote against the pact, concluded under the General Agreement on Thriffs and TTade. Mr. Brown passed a 12-question test on the complex accord prepared by consumer activist and GATT foe Ralph Nader. Mr. Nader and an unusual coalition of liberals and conservatives have argued that few in Congress have read the text of the trade deal, which they say will hurt certain U.S. industries, expand the federal budget deficit, and submit U.S. environmental, safety and health laws to an international trade bureaucracy. The Colorado senator decided to switch sides while cramming for Mr. Nader's quiz. "After having gone through it, my mind has been changed," Mr. Brown said. "What I find is an agreement that establishes an in- ternational court system . . . without due process." At yesterday's White House rally, Mr. Clinton, Vice President Al Gore and Republican luminaries such as former Secretary of State James A. Baker I I I and Reagan administration budget chief James C. Miller put forward a number of arguments in favor of the deal, which supporters say will boost U.S. exports and lower consumer prices at home. The White House also released the text of a letter signed by former Presidents Carter, Ford and Bush, all urging ratification of GATT, reminiscent of a similar lobbying blitz for last year's North American Free Urade Agreement. Mr. Clinton went out of his way to underscore the bipartisan support for GATT, linking election survival to congressional approval of the accord. The agreement "not only tears down trade barriers, it bulldozes differences of party," he said. Mr. Clinton's unusual focus on the impact that GATT would have on blue-collar Americans comes on the heels of two recent promises to look out for "ordinary Americans" and appears aimed at defusing complaints from liberals that his new shift to the political center was alienating the traditional base of Democratic voters. His direct reference to the economic woes of "non-collegeeducated male workers" — the largest group of voters to reject the president in recent polls — was also apparently meant to mollify big labor's hostility to GATT, which unions fear will lead to fewer American jobs and declining wages as corporations look overseas for cheaper labor. Mr. Clinton gathered dozens of current and former administration officials dating as far back as the Thiman administration to join iri the final push for the agreement, which has been the subject of negotiations dating back to the Reagan administration. Mr. Baker, secretary of state under President Bush, said the country "once again faces a momentous choice." "We really face a choice between escapism or engagement." Mr. Baker said that delay on voting for GATT, as sought by conservatives and liberals alike, "could prove fatal." He added that "the world will correctly read any rejection of GATT as an American retreat from intemationai leadership." Mr. Miller, who headed the Office of Management and Budget in the mid-1980s, attacked arguments that the trade agreement will break the budget and sacrifice American sovereignty. "In my judgment, this would not be a budget buster," he said, while noting that the OMB and the Congressional Budget Office agree that losses from tariff reductions would be made up through expanded trade. Separately, Sen. Howard M. Metzenbaum, Ohio Democrat, and Sen. Byron L. Dorgan, North Dakota Democrat, both announced plans to vote against GATT. Both had been expected to oppose the accord. Mr. Dorgan said Senate sentiment on the make-or-break budget vote was still extraordinarily fluid. "This is a much more difficult vote to cast," he said. " I don't quite know where the votes are." �GALLERY National Gallery 'disaster' feared Report says art needs better care By Rod Dreher THE WSHINQTON TIMES A confidential report blasts the National Gallery of Art for putting the museum's entire collection at risk of damage and "emphatically" urges administrators to correct the problems "before disaster occurs." Problems cited by a team of independent evaluators include deficient facilities, substandard climate-control equipment and poorly trained staffers: The 99-page document, a copy of which was obtained by The Washington Times, also found widespread organizational problems, poor staff morale and a lack of fiscal accountability. A member of the House subcommittee that oversees funding for the gallery said he was "astounded" by the report. Rep. Ralph Regula, Ohio Republican, who is in line to head the House Appropriations Interior subcommittee, said yesterday he would push for hearings to call gallery administrators to account for the lapse. The collection includes more than 75,000 artworks from the 12th to the 20th centuries, including masterpieces by Titian, da Vinci, Rembrandt and van Gogh. The gallery also plays host to exhibits on loan from other museums from around the world. Its S55 million annual budget — onethird that of the entire National Endowment for the Arts — covers the cost of operating the museum but does not fund acquisitions, which are accomplished by donation only. see GALLERY, page A14 From page Al Working under the auspices of the Association of Higher Education Facihties Officers, the team of experts reviewed the gallery's facilities in March at the request of the building's administrators. They found "extertsive" leaking from skylights and the roof, as well as moisture entering the building through the facade. Ttemperature and humidity affect the extremely fragile state of old paintings. Maintaining constant temperature and moisture conditions is vital to the integrity of artworks, which is why most galleries use expensive climate monitoring and control systems to protect their priceless collections. "The building's mechanical systems are operated in a 'by-theseat-of-the-pants manner,'" the report says. Unsafe temperature and humidity situations "are commonplace and could easily exceed poorly trained workers' abilities to bring such excursions under control before serious damage is done to the artwork." Ruth Kaplan, the gallery's public information officer, said plans were in place to correct many of the problems cited in the report even before it came out. She said the gallery will begin installation of a new climate-control system next year. It will not be completed, however, until 1997. "The existing system is operational," she said. "It has been functioning. It's labor-intensive in that it requires a lot of manpower. The system is not dysfunctional." She said the running of the present system "has always been very tight" and denied that the gallery's collection is now or has ever been at risk. "The National Gallery's collection is fully protected and not in any danger." Yet the report states that until the new system is up and running, "the Gallery's collection is at risk from continuing temperature and humidity excursions." At least two -such situations already have occurred, the report said, but it did not elaborate. WJLA-TV (Channel 7), which broke the story last night, reported that a display room containing paintings filled with steam one day last winter when the heating system malfunctioned. "The walls were wet like someone had sprayed them with a fine mist," maintenance worker James Edwards told Channel 7. "And the paintings had signs of oxidization — you know, turning white." Among the paintings affected was John Singleton Copley's "Watson and the Shark" (1778), one of the most significant works by Colonial America's greatest portrait painter. " I was told not to say anything, not to tell anybody and not to come back in that area," Mr. Edwards said. Miss Kaplan called the incident "a temporary problem which was immediately corrected." "There was no damage sustained to any of the pictures. In the place where this happened, the pictures were immediately vacated," she said. She added that "to the best of our knowledge, there has never been a restriction placed on conversation by any supervisor to an employee." The second incident was reported in a gallery memorandum dated Oct. 13 and obtained by The Times. It details the discovery of sections of plaster falling "dangerously close to a number of works of art" kept in two storerooms. The memo to Facilities Manager Gregg Reynolds was sent by Sally Freitag in NGA Director Earl "Rusty" Powell Ill's office and by Gordon Anson, who oversees design and installation for the museum. Upon further investigation of the ceiling space above the storerooms, gallery administrators found "a lot of old water damage as well as a bucket which contained water." "Since the Art Storage Rooms contain a large concentration of art (1,000 paintings)," the memo reads, "these areas should be treated with the same degree of concem as the Gallery spaces. It appears this is not the case in these storerooms, and if we had not investigated the problem, there could have been damage to the art in these spaces." According to the report, operr ations engineers regulate the temperature in gallery rooms manually, by turning up the Jieat full blast when a room is too cold or mnning the air conditioner at the coldest setting if a room is too warm. Operating the air handling units from one extreme to the other "is hard on equipment and very dependent upon skilled and knowledgeable operators," the report says. The independent report paints a portrait of a top-heavy bureaucracy riven with administrative disorganization, singling out for criticism Mr. Reynolds and his superior, Deputy Administrator Darrell Willson. The report found maintenance of the gallery's mechanical and electrical system to be "minimal." Investigators said the same building operations staff charged with taking care of routine maintenance is also required to support special projects and events, without regard to the effect on maintenance operations or budgets. "Since these costs are just absorbed as part of doing business, no records and no accountability exist," the report states. Mr. Regula intends to hold congressional hearings to find out why things have gone wrong at the gallery. " I can't believe they'd let this happen. It just astounds me," he said. 0(1 �Clmton's credibility up in air President riding unpredictable currents of trade winds By Donald Lambro THE WASHINGTON TIMES Once again the stakes for President Clinton in this week's congressional vote on the General Agreement on Thriffs and TVade are as high as they can get: his political credibility. As with so many down-to-thewire legislative battles he has fought over the course of his presidency, Mr. ClinivjTT'V^nc; ton faces yet anl^r^Yra her do-oi^die A N A I Y S I S scenario. If he fails to win House and Senate approval of the intemationai trade accord, his administration will suffer a serious blow to its prestige both at home and abroad. I f he wins, he will have emerged from another hard-fought trade battle, but not without an embarrassing political struggle and the last-minute help of his Republican opponents. "After what happened this month [in the elections], he needs this one badly," said a Democratic Party official. "A loss on GATT would be a disastrous blow in the postelection period," said Jerry Jasinowski, president of the National Association of Manufacturers. "It would reduce his leverage on both international and domestic issues." Politically weakened after the massive defeat his party suffered in the midterm elections, Mr. Clinton cannot afford another political rejection, especially from a lameduck Congress still controUed by Democrats. With his job approval scores stuck in the inid-40s, Mr. Clinton is trying to reposition himself in the poUtical mainstream, and centrist Democrats think a victory for the worldwide free-trade agreement will help him do that. "One of the high points of his presidency was his victory on the North American Free TVade Agreement, which is a New Democrat position," said Al From, the president of the Democratic Leadership Council. "We think that's where the future of our party is." ot In the end, say pro-GATT supporters, the outcome will be determined by House and Senate Republicans who overwhelmingly support free-trade policies. For Mr. From, the "old Democrat position" on trade is represented by the party's left wing and its protectionist allies in organized labor opposed both to NAFTA and GATT. He sees the Clinton position on trade as a historic "break with the past" that will help move the party back to the center. This week's vote in the House and Senate is in many respects a replay of last November's bitter battle over NAFTA, which also was a test of Mr. Clinton's political credibility at a time when he was sagging in the polls. Then, as now, Mr. Clinton was faced with'opposition from such strange bedfellows as the AFLCIO, Ralph Nader, Greenpeace and the Sierra Club on the left and conservative commentator Pat Buchanan and Ttexas billionaire Ross Perot on the right. The issue theii,' as now, was between those who wanted to lower trade tariffs to encourage international trade and those who want to raise tariffs to protect vulnerable U.S. industries and jobs from foreign competition. The poUtical fight over NAFTA deeply divided the Democrats, embittered some of the president's strongest supporters in organized labor and triggered threats of political retribution from labor leaders against those who voted against them. Eventually the administration and labor, renewed their alliance over health care and other measures, and the unions threw their support to Democratic i n cumbents and challengers. But Democratic strategists said yesterday that the opposition to GATT is not as deeper as intense as it was in the NAFTA dispute. One sign of the change is the support of House Democratic leader Richard A. Gephardt of Missouri, who was a fierce NAFTA opponent. " I dont think a whole lot of people are paying a lot of attention to GATT," said a Democratic Party strategist. "We don't see a whole lot of money being spent by the special interests lobbying for or against it, as you did with NAFTA." "There's not as much controversy or debate over GATT as there was over NAFTA, and there is more bipartisan support," said Ed Lazarus, chief of strategic communications at the Democratic National Committee. "It was not raised as an issue in many election campaigns. I don't see as much rancor over it this time." Nevertheless, the White House pulled out all the stops yesterday to show bipartisan support for the agreement, releasing a letter of support from former Presidents Ford,'Cairter and Bush and joining forces with promment GOP supporters of GATT. Privately, some Democrats criticize the administration for not buUding earlier support for GATT and waiting too long to mount a lobbying campaign. "They didnt leam their lesson with NAFTA and started too late," said a Democratic campaign strategist who requested anonymity. In the end, say pro-GATT supporters, the outcome will be determined by House and Senate Republicans who overwhelmingly support free-trade policies. "You're going to see more Republicans voting for GAIT in the House than the Democrats," Mr. Jasinowski said. Patricia Schroeder, Colorado Democrat, is the senior woman in Congress, she is likely to come up empty-handed next month when the Democrats dole out their ranking committee positions. Mrs. Schroeder, who won a 12th term Nov. 8, was set to take over as top Democrat on the Post Office and Civil Service Committee. But Republicans plan-to abolish that committee, folding it into Government Operations. So Mrs. Schroeder instead has set her sights on the Judiciary Committee, where six of the top eight Democrats retired or were defeated this year, including Chairman Jack Brooks of Ibxas. But she'll have to beat Rep. John Conyers Jr., Michigan Democrat and the senior black in Congress, to get there, and committee members say that's unlikely. CONGRESSOSIAL QmKTERIY "I'm obviously in line," said Mrs. Schroeder, who maintains that because Mr. Conyers already chairs Govemment Operations, he is out of contention for Judiciary. But Mr. Conyers plans to relinquish the ranking post at Govemment Operations in favor of the Judiciary post. In a letter to his colleagues last week, Mr. Conyers said that just as he succeeded Mr. Brooks as chairman of Govemment Operations, he is ready to follow Mr. Brooks on Judiciary. " I will seek to be a consensus builder in our Democratic caucus," Mr. Conyers wrote. Gen. Charles Link, Air By Rowan Scarborough THE WASHINQTON TIMES Force special assistant on roles and missions, in a Nov. 21 letter to the commission. "Therefore, I wish to withdraw the classified and unclassified versions of the Overseas Presence Issue Papers." The request to withdraw the position paper marked a retreat by the Air Force in an increasingly bitter behind-the-scenes struggle among the four miUtary branches. The commission, which includes retired mUitary brass, former politicians and corporate leaders, was created by Congress last year to sort out the myriad roles perforated by each branch. Its recommendations on abolishing or consolidating future roles are to be presented in May to Defense Secretary WiUiam Perry and to Congress. With defense budgets smaUer in the post-Soviet era, the services view the commission's findings as important to their futures. Since the summer, each has bombarded the panel with written and oral presentations arguing why it should take over the others' roles and missions. The Navy, for example, wants to assume the Army's role in ballistic-missUe defense. The Army is questioning the need for the Air Force's futuristic stealth fighter, the F-22. But the Air Force by far has been the most combative presenter. In papers prepared under the guidance of Gen. McPeak, an ex-fighter pUot whose four-year tenure ended last month, the Air Force criticized some of the other branches' most cherished projects. It said the Army should scrap ATACMs (Army Tactical Advanced Missile System) because ballistic missiles would interfere with Air Force flight operations behind enemy Unes. The Air Force said Marine Corps aviation should be transferred to the Navy. And it recommended the Navy tenninate its future carrier strike plane, the F-18E/F in favor of "more advanced, stealthy aircraft" — i.e. the F-22. The McPeak papers have infuriated the other branches, but none more than the Navy. This was the backdrop to the Air Force submission on naval forces that prompted Adm. Boorda to complain. Opinion polls indicate no single party will be able to form a govemm^^m^—^~m ment by itself, Tyrpwc so deadlock and i^JLVra economic paraA N A I Y S K lysis are likely to continue in that "forgotten nation" of 9 mUlion. While politicians hurl insults at each other and people complain of the growing poverty and skyrocketing crime, Bulgarian diplomats say anti-Americanism is on the rise. For no reason except convenience and old, Cold War habits, Washington is getting blamed for Bulgaria's misfortunes, with Communists who call themselves Socialists poised to exploit the growing national despondency. President Zhelyu Zhelev, a former anti-Communist dissident, dissolved parliament Oct. 17 and called early elections. He attributes the success of the SociaUsts to their exploitation of nationalistic feeling. "We have to admit that the Socialist Party succeeded, and that it used the time to solidify its position," Mr. Zhelev said. In a statement assessing the forthcoming elections, he bluntly described the five years that followed Communism's coUapse in Bulgaria as wasted. "We all had many iUusions," said the head of the now-splintered Union of Democratic Forces. "We thought that the changes would come about more quickly, much more easUy, more smoothly. "But reality turned out to be more cruel and much more severe," Mr Zhelev added. "And we are now paying for our iUusions." Bulgaria trails behind most former East bloc countries with its reforms, most of which have become victims of political in- fighting and efforts of the exCommunists to keep their former influence. According to former parliament Speaker Alexander Yordanov, Bulgaria's politicians led the country away from its totalitarian past but put nothing in its place to fulfill the people's expectations. "Weak laws and poor social security resulted in a miserable existence and gradually turned Bulgaria into a kingdom of swindlers," he said. Foreign diplomats suggest that unless the government curbs crime, speculation and theft of public property, next month's elections will be meaningless. President Zhelev has publicly agreed with this assessment but has failed to generate an atmosphere conducive to democratic elections and reforms. Although his govemment initiaUy privatized a number of stateowned firms and favored the creation of small businesses, lack of a coherent long-term economic policy soon began taking its toll. The annual inflation rate is hovering around 100 percent, small enterprises are targeted by racketeers and workers in many factories are not paid for months at a time. Although most state farms have been abandoned, the retum of the land to its previous owners has lagged. Agricultural production, once Bulgaria's pride, has plummeted. Formerly a major exporter of canned food, the country recently started importing fruit from its neighbors. Some of the country's problems are blamed on the intemationai sanctions against Yugoslavia, Bulgaria's neighbor to the west. The U.N. Security Council sanctions were eased sUghtly this faU, but Bulgaria says it has lost $2 billion in trade because of the sanctions and has asked the United Nations — without success — for compensation. Bulgarian diplomats also complain that the major powers pay little attention to their country, concentrating mainly on Russia and, to a lesser extent, on the three Reuters Bulgarian President Zhelyu Zhelev, cheered by Sofia residents at the polls in 1992, has dissolved parliament and called early elections. m l l e t "Weak laws and poor social security resulted in a miserable existence and gradually turned Bulgaria into a kingdom of swindlers' "I 100 ROMANIA Bucharest* SERBIA) BULGARIA • Sofia MACEDONIA TURKEY former East bloc countries in the forefront of reforms: Hungary, Poland and the Czech Republic. She says Bulgaria's expectations after communism's collapse "have not been met" by the Westem countries, which showed "no Atlantic adequate reaction to human feelOcean ings." Term limits The term-limits argument today in the Supreme Court stems from the anger of some political outsiders about Democratic dominance in Arkansas. " I came to believe that Arkansas has the most rigged political system in the entire country because it's so one-sided, so narrow, 50 lacking in real competition," said contractor Skip Cook. Mr. Cook told the Philadelphia Inquirer's Aaron Epstein that he quit his job as a stockbroker, depleted his savings and "lived like a pauper" so he could campaign for term limits against career politicians of any party. Aided by Washington-based U.S. Term Limits, he stunned the big boys. Sixty percent of Arkansas voters backed a 1992 state constitutional amendment to limit the number of times a politician can be re-elected. Clinton fund sinking? The Philadelphia Inquirer's Donna St. George reports that something happened to President Clinton's legal defense fund. Clinton supporter and lobbyist Jan Schoonmaker says, " I think it's kind of dropped off the map." Out of 75 persons interviewed, only one admits opening his wallet: Jody Powell, who was press secretary to President Carter. Mr. Powell is not saying how much he contributed to the president to fight his legal battles. Meanwhile, the fund has been sued by two conservative groups that contend it is unlawful. That means the fund has to raise more money to pay its own legal bills before it gets around to shelling out for the president. Thafs because GATT itself is a trade agreement that makes trade among the 117 signatory nations freer but retains many management tools to protect potentially vulnerable industries in the United States, Europe and elsewhere. GATT would reduce tariffs worldwide by 40 percent and bring key elements of the U.S. economy— agriculture, inteUectual property and services—under world trading rules. This facet of GATT wiU open many previously closed markets to U.S. goods and services. All of which makes GATT an attractive trade pact to both RepubUcans and Democrats. The debate surrounding it has never reached the volume or intensity of the debate over the North American Free Ttade Agreement, and poUtical opposition has been comparably muted. Nevertheless, GATT's passage is not assured and the reasons have less to do with the merits of freer world trade than with these blunt poUtical questions: • How does GATT affect campaigns for the 1996 RepubUcan presidential nomination? • WiU RepubUcans,freshfrom their historic victories in the midterm elections, cooperate with the Clinton administration on a high-visibiUty economic issue? • Can GATT rehabiUtate Presi- dent Clinton's image in the after- cies should he run for president in math of a disasterous midterm elec- 1996: conservatives worried that tion? Outgoing House Majority Lead er Richard A. Gephardt of Missouri supports GATT and has lent his authoritative voice within the 267member House Democratic caucus to the pro-GATT forces. House passage is virtually assured, leaving GATTs fate in the hands of the Senate. A majority of senators support GATT, but the treaty must attract a minimum of 60 votes because Senate rules require a 60-vote nuuority to waive the budget act, which GATT does. The budget act requires Congress to pay for aU tax cuts with equivalent reductions in federal spending. Budget analysts estimate GATT wiU cost the H-easury some $12 bUUon in lost tariffrevenuedue to thej reduction or elimination of trade tariffs. Congress and the administration have come up with $12 bUUon in savings, but that covers only five years of the estimated lost revenue. Senate rules require Congress to come up with budget savings to cover tariff losses for the entire decade. Before the Senate can vote on the merits of GAIT itself, it must vote to waive that requirement. Waiving Senate rulesrequiresa 60-vote majority, meaning support of Mr. Dole and other RepubUcans is crucial to GATT's survival. �By David R. Sands THE WASHINGTON TIMES T here's no general agreement on the General Agreement on Thriffs and TVade. A complex, 126-nation compact that was seven years in the negotiating, GATT's Uruguay Round faces a make-orbreak vote in Congress this week. Behind the mind-numbing jargon and conflicting cost-benefit analyses, the GATT debate reveals the seismic shift in the U.S. trade debate: the breakdown ofan American consensusforfreertrade and open international markets. Thefirstsigns of a bitter split in opimon on U.S. trade policy came during last year's firestorm over the North American Free "frade Agreement Tb illustrate how far apart the opposing sides are, con. sider these statements: • "GATT is worthfiveNAFTAs," U-easury Secretary Lloyd Bentsen said. • "GAIT is 100 times worse than NAFTA," consumer advocate Ralph Nader said. When the Senate last voted on a GATT pact — the Tbkyo Round in 1979—the vote was 90-4. This time, the Clinton administration is scrambling to assemble the 60 Senate votes needed to assure the pact's passage. Once primarily a mutual tariffcutting exercise, GATT has become a victim of its own success. While supporters tout significant cuts in import taxes around the world, the GATT controversy has focused on issues that were never on the trade agenda before: trade in services, patent and copyright protection, government procurement rules, the environment, worker tute for International Economics, a Washington think tank, put the benefit to the U.S. economy at $65 bUlion a year by 2004, or an extra 1 percent a year in GDP growth. GATT critics cite a study by the nonpartisan U.S. International Ttade Commission in June. It found that 38 of 58 U.S. industries studied would see modest gains in exports of less than 5 percent from GATT, 13 would see minor losses, and one — textiles and apparel —would suffer losses of 15 percent or more. Only the U.S. pharmaceutical industry would see gains in exports of more than 15 percent, according to the ITC analysis. The GATT accord has proved Again led by the United States, controversial abroad as weU. GATT Executive Director Peter Sutherland, the Irishman credited with a key role in bringing the faltering Uruguay Round talks to a conclusion last December, says the U.S. vote wiU essentially determine the success or faUure of the accord. The GATT vote also comes at a crucial time for Mr. Clinton, already reeling from the Democratic loss of control in Congress in the elections this month. Mr. Clinton recently obtained a pledge from key East Asian nations to move toward free trade and investment in the Pacific by 2020, and he travels to Miami next month for a trade summit with Latin American leaders. Administration officials talk of a trade "triple play" that would boost U.S. exports and Mr. CUnton's prestige. GATT supporters say the vote must be taken by the end of the year or else the package could unravel. Mr. Clinton requested the extraordinary lame-duck sessions of both houses of Congress in part because putting off the vote until next year would almost certainly leave the treaty open to deal-killing amendments. But even thefightto get a vote on GATT reveals the unresolved division on trade. Unhappy with Clinton administration requests to include environmental and labor issues in future trade accords, congressional Republicans managed to stripfromthe GATT bUI the "fasttrack authority" used by the executive branch to negotiate trade agreements. Mr. Clinton thusfindshimself I in the unusual position of pushing through a major agreement on trade while lacking the authority to negotiate the next one. �GATT AT A GLANCE ROUND BY ROUND A history of the General Agreement on Tariffs and TVade 1947 — Geneva Round — Twenty-three nations participate. 1949 — Annecy Round 19SO-51 — Torquay Round 1955-56 — First Geneva Round 1960-62 — Second Geneva Round, also known as the Dillon Round 1964-67 — Kennedy Round, including new rules against dumping exports 1973-79 — Tokyo Round, which first looked at non tariff barriers 1986 — Uruguay Round launched in Punta del Este, Uruguay, with 126 nations eventually participating. New areas include services, investment, and agriculture. 1990-92 — Negotiators miss repeated deadlines to conclude talks. Dec. 15,1993 — Agreement struck on Uruguay Round deal in Geneva. April 15,1994 —GATT accord signed in Marrakesh, Morocco Oct. 5,1994 — House Joins Senate in putting off GATT vote until after election Nov: 29,1994 — House vote on GATT Doc. 1,1994 — Senate vote on GATT Jan. 1,1995 — Proposed World Trade Organization begins operations TheVtasNngton-nmn Key points ot the Uruguay Round, concluded April 15, 1994 • Cuts overall import duties by about 40 percent on thousands of products. • Phases out import limits that raise prices of textiles and clothing. • Offers new protections for toreign investment. • Sets new rules to open up government procurement to foreign bidders. • Scales back government state supports to farmers. • Boosts protection for copyrights and patents. • Sets up new rules to help liberalize trade in services such as tourism, engineering, and education • Establishes a new World Trade Organization to replace the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade, or GATT, that was set up in 1948. • Takes effect Jan. 1,1995, if United States and other big trading nations ratify accords this year. Sands THE WASHINGTON TIMES T he success of GATT would be the death of "GATT." If the CUnton administration has its way, one ofthe world's clunkiest acronyms wiU be history soon after Congress ratifies the massive trade pact. With the creation of the new World "Ihide Organization, or WTO, administration officials say they wiU quickly move to withdraw from the General Agreement on Tkriffs and H-ade, the stopgap non-organization that has been by default the principal vehicle for freer trade since the end of World War II. The original GATT accord, in 1947, envisioned an "International Ttade Organization" designed to take its place alongside the International Monetary Fund and the World Bank in the triumvirate of organizations to oversee the global economy. But owing largely to U.S. sovereignty concerns, the ITO never came into being, and trade liberalization has been conducted instead through eight "rounds" of negotiations between 1947 and 1994, conducted under the principles enshrined in GATT. CUnton administration officials announced earUer this month that the United States plans to withdraw from GATT early next year, assuming the treaty passes Congress this week and the World TVade Organi- zation is up and running as scheduled by Jan. 1. Under GATT rules, any contracting member can withdraw from the agreement after giving 60 days' notice. The change could be mean more than just ordering new stationery. U.S. officials say the decision to leave GATT has little practical significance, but trading partners in Europe and Japan have expressed alarm that leaving GATT compUcates the resolution of some longstanding trade disputes working their way through the cumbersome GATT bureaucracy. Andrew Stoler, U.S. deputy trade ambassador to GATT, said the United States wiU not renege on obUgations it has from past GATT decisions, but stiU-pending cases such as a Mexican chaUenge to U.S. dolphin protection laws apparently wiU simply lapse. GATT officials had been hoping for a two-year transition when both the WTO and GATT would coexist. Other major trading nations may foUow the U.S. example, forcing the WTO to start afresh on a wide range of trade disputes. Further complicating matters, some nations, now members in good standing in GATT, may not make the Jan. 1 deadUne to join the WTO. Less than a third of the 126 nations that have signed the Uruguay Round accord have formaUy ratified the treaty, and at least one major trader — Switzerland — has said it won't be able to complete its ratification by Jan. 1. 03 �PAGE-E2 / TUESDAY, NOVEMBER 29, 1994 • Stye Pagfrmafam gimcg GATT DEBATE "World Trade Organization' a hot-button name By David R. Sands THE WASHINGTON TIMES F or an institution with the most bland of names, the World Ttade Organization has managed to inspire some pretty heated passions. Tb conservative columnist Pat Buchanan, the WTO is "a pillar of the New World Order." But to Joe Cobb, economicsfellowat the conservative Heritage Foundation, the WTO "promises to bring to the world economy the essential system of the rule of law." For liberal consumer activist Ralph Nader, the WTO would be "a grave threat to the improvements in noncommercial living standards won in recent decades by citizen activists, especially in the United States but also around the world." For U.S. TY-ade Representative Mickey Kantor, "the question is whether the WTO will affect our sovreignty and the answer is clearly no." Nothing else in the domestic debate over the GATT trade accord has inspired such emotion or provided so much radio talk-show fodder as the World Ttade Organization, which is something of a surprise because it was US. negotiatiors who pushed hardest in the 8-year negotiations for a strenghthened WTO. A Uttle history is needed to put the clash in perspective. In the years just after World War I I , U.S. and British economists proposed three major multinational organizations to oversee the postwar economic reconstruction: The World Bank would provide development funding for war-ravaged economies, the International Monetary Fund would monitor currency flows, and the International Ttade Organization would oversee international trade. They got two out of three. While the proposed ITO was being debated, 23 countries also approved a temporary "General Agreement on Thriffs and Ttade" to start the business of cutting tariffs and other trade barriers. Owing largely to US. fears, the ITO never came into being, and the stopgap, voluntary GAIT became by default the vehicle for eight "rounds" of trade Uberalization. But U.S. exporters have long complained that GATT was more cheerleader than rulemaker, and countries that didn't Uke a particular decision to open a market or lower a trade barrier could simply ignore GATT rulings. Under the current GATT system, for example, a country that loses a case before a GATT "dispute panel" can sunply veto the decision. Contracting members of GATT — which in time topped 120 countries — also could take waivers on any rules they chose, one reason why agriculture has been largely off the table in the postwar freetrade movement. The WTO would replace the old GATT secretariat, and member countries wiU be face much tougher hurdles in opting out of WTO commitments, including new cuts in tariffs, opening services markets, and other reforms. The Genevabased WTO would also become a permanent forum for refereeing trade disputes and pushing for chaUenged as unfair trade barriers, freer trade. A cruel joke! Usually politicians who win elections in November at least wait until January before they start betraying you on behalf of their corporate masters. Mr. Gingrich started doing that on Nov. 2 with his support of U.S. membership in the World Ttade Organization (WTO) during the upcoming lame-duck session of Congress. With Mr. Gingrich leading RepubUcans for the WTO, the future House speaker shifts power from the states and Washing- ton to Geneva, Switzerland; goes along with the Senate, which has to bust its budgetdeficit-control law by $31 bilUon before it can vote on the WTO; and makes possible the foreign regulation of America. Here is how the GATT-WTO agreement with 118 nation members tempts Mr. Gingrich to say one thing and do the opposite. He wants the United States to become a member with only one vote and no veto bower. Any two Uttle dictatorships overseas can outvote the United States. The Bush administration opposed the WTO for just this reason in December 1992. In addition, Mr. Gingrich is supporting the WTO's secret tribunals, whose trade judges can rule for a foreign country's claim that our federal, state and local laws are restricting imports because of our generaUy higher health, safety and workplace Robert T. Matsui Interdependence is here I n a world punctuated by uncertainty, one thing is clear. The nations of the world are becoming increasingly interdependent. There are some who think that's a bad idea, and they are doing everything they can to dispute the evidence that growing intemationai trade can create a world that is increasingly both peaceful and prosperous. These opponents have locked onto the General Agreement on Thriffs and Ttade (GATT) as the embodiment of a shrinking world. They have transformed the GATT debate from a discussion of the economics offreetrade into a harangue about U.S. sovereignty, sweetheart deals and the loss of American jobs. The opponents argue that a job gained in another country is a job lost in the United States. They refute the idea that aU countries benefit when each competesfreelyin a fair market by doing what each does best. They started the divisive attacks last year during the congressional review of the North American Free Ttade Agreement (NAFTA). Though NAFTA was approved, the arguments against free trade began taking root. But all the ranting from soapboxes doesn't make the opponents' arguments tme. A look at the GATT shows it is an economic winner for the United States. Liberal and conservative economists agree that ratification of the agreement wiU add $100 to $200 bilUon to the U.S. economy each year, create thousands of jobs and lower the federal deficit. The internationalfreetrade agreement is supported by presidents from Ronald Reagan to BUI Clinton, by organizationsfromthe Consumers Union to the Heritage Fbundation and by leadership associations from the National Governors Association to the National Association of Attorneys General. But the fight over GATT has moved far from the battlefield-sized common ground. Opponents on the far left and far right have tapped effectively into fears of people across the country about job insecurity and the future. Although the economy is strong and unemployment is low, Americans see layoffs, corporate downsizing and stagnant wages. Economic uncertainty heightens concerns about competition from low-paid workers in developing countries, but the reaUty is that those countries wiU be the best consumers of American-made products. By the year 2010, developing countries are expected to import more goods than the European Economic Community and Japan combined. If we are going to claim our share of those growing markets, we must extend our reach to those nations. GATT's successor, the World Ttade Organization (WTO), has unfairly become a lightning rod for those who fear broader trade and intemationai contact In arguments that echo those made against American membership in the United Nations, opponents decry a loss of American sovereignty. Yet the United States was the leading advocate for the WTO. We are the largest trading country in the world and bring the most cases against other countries for violations of international trade laws. Underthe GATT dispute-resolution system, the United States and other members can veto the decisions of arbitration boards. Under the WTO, that veto would be eUminated. We pushed for that change because the European Union and other countries have failed to comply with the decisions when we have won, and we want to prevent that from happening in the future. Nations have been fighting about the rules since trade has existed. The challenge Ues in creating rules that aUow such fights to be resolved in an equitable and peaceful way. The existing GAIT rules have served us for five decades and have gone a long way toward achieveing those goals. The WTO advances that process. StiU, there are those who argue that the GATT is fuU of sweetheart deals and benefits for a privUeged few. Several members of the Senate, where a procedural vote could sink the GATT, continue to raU about sovereignty, gains for media corporations and losses for American workers. What they don't mention is poUtics. There are U.S. senators who are trying to shore up the walls of protectionism that keep prices artificiaUy highfortheir regional industries — a move that hurts consumers across the country. Others have presidential aspirations and may be using their opposition to the GAIT as a poUtical tool for their presidential campaigns. And there are those who, with the changes sparked by the most recent election, are looking for ways to use their new power. They seem to revel in making the administration squirm by threatening to kiU — or stall indefinitely — the GATT. None of the arguments — the perceived loss of sovereignty, the wUdly exaggerated threat to U.S. workers or the self-interests of individual lawmakers — are justifiable reasons to oppose the GATT. The GATT will save each American family about $110 a year by cutting taxes on goods; it wiU create hundreds of thousands of jobs at home and abroad; and it will call on more than 120 countries to work together for a more peaceful and prosperous world. The opponents of GATT are worried about a world that is shrinking with each intemationai telephone call, each overseas fax and each trans-Atlantic airplane flight. But the opponents can't turn back the clock and simply close our country to the outside world, as much as they may like to try. We cannot let them throw sand in the gears of the engine that can drive the United States into a better tomorrow. Rep. Robert T. Matsui, Democrat from Sacramento, Calif., is the acting chairman of the Trade Subcommittee of the U. S. House of Representatives. I �Charles W. Steel tariffs will be eliminated, which important step toward trade expansion and ' Not trade hysteria, but U.S. leadership W ithin a few days, the Senate and the House are scheduled to vote on the legislation needed to implement the GATT trade agreement. This is unquestionably one of the most important bills to come before the 103rd Congress. It is also one of the most unexpectedly controversial. Opponents of trade liberalization have bombarded the American public and their legislators with assertions that they have nothing to gain and a great deal to lose from implementation of the GATT trade agreement: U.S. sovereignty will be endangered, they say; our environmental and health standards will go down; workers' rights will be abandoned; and unemployment will soar. Frightening words! But these arguments carry about as much weight as NAFTA's w trade Uberalization. It represents a highly commendable achievement in strengthening the multilateral trading system. Now that the polluted pre-election atmosphere has cleared, legislators are free to vote their consciences on this issue. For some, it wiU be their last legislative responsibUity. For others, this vote wiU set the stage for future coUaborative action by the next Congress on matters that affect every American citizen and the country as a whole. In either case, this is a valuable opportunity for all legislators to demonstrate leadership at home and to set an example for the rest of the trading world. Doreen L Brown is president of Consumers for World Ttade. Lawrence Chimerine No exaggeration: just modest benefits for U.S. companies M any members of Congress, soon to consider passage of the Uruguay Round GATT Agreement, may find their decision complicated by the typical exaggerations that characterize most debates on trade poUcy. Proponents are overestimating the benefits ofthe GATT, whUe opponents claim the agreement will devastate the U.S. economy. As is usual, the truth Ues somewhere in between. In my view, based on an industryby-industry analysis, the Uruguay Round Agreement wiU generate measurable, but modest, benefits overall, although some industries wiU clearly be hurt. These benefits wiU result from the m^jor changes negotiated by the CUnton administration to the Dunkel draft of the agreement inherited from the previous administration. In that version, modest increases for U.S. agriculture and service industries would have been overwhelmed by large losses across a broad range of manufacturing industries. By contrast, this new agreement will provide benefits for several major sectors of our economy, including the foUowing: •Exports of US. agricultural products wiU rise because ofreductionsin export subsidies and tariffs in Europe, as weU as requirements for minimum import access in aU countries. •Fbreign tariffs on telecommunications equipment printers, paper and wood products wiU be lowered and tariffs on construction equipment, medical equipment, steel, pharmaceuticals, toys, and furniture will be phased out, thereby bolstering exports of these products. High-technology goods wiU be especiaUy helped. •Provisions regarding aircraft subsidies wiU help the U.S. aircraft industry in its competitive battle with Airbus and other foreign producers. •Stronger protection of inteUectual prop- erty wiU help those U.S. industries that have sufferedfrompiracy or product counterfeiting, especiaUy the software and pharmaceutical industries. At the same time, however, other factors will work against U.S. trade and economic activity. Fbr example, the textile and apparel industries are the sacrificial lambs of the agreement — the phasing out of the MultiFiber Arrangement wiU result in a huge acceleration of imports at the expense of domestic production, displacing hundreds of thousands of workers. A wide range of nontariff trade barriers (e.g., discriminatory govemment procurement, lack of access to distribution channels) scarcely touched by the Uruguay Round wiU continue limiting U.S. exports to many countries. The agreement wiU also weaken U.S. trade laws somewhat, eventuaUy leading to more iUegal dumping of foreign goods into U.S. markets, and reducing our leverage in opening foreign markets. On balance, therefore, the agreement is clearly not the greatest thing since sUced principles. bread, despite optimistic projections by its Otherwise, the advocates. On the other hand, even the United States modest net gains of $25 biUion to $30 bU- wiU suffer Uon a year should not be ignored, especiaUy from a widentrade since much of the added economic activity ing wiU be concentrated in the high value- deficit with added, high-productivity, high-wage indus- China much tries that produce the types of jobs this as it has with Japan, offseteconomy badly needs. gains Thus, despite itsflaws,I urge members of ting Congress to vote in favor of the agreement: from the new agreement Third, the movement toward free trade It is also important that Congress and the administration address other issues to in other Asian countries, discussed at the ensure the US. economy reaps the modest recent Asia Pacific Economic Conference benefits I expect. Every day that goes by without Congressional ratification of the GATT implementing legislation postpones the pact's enormous economic advantages for American businesses, workers and consumers. And prospects for its ratification will be slim—at best — if Congress defers action on the measure until next year. We cant waitforGATT. SincetiieGATT process began in 1947, industrialized nations have cut average tariffs from 40 to 5 percent, thereby contributing to the fastest global economic growth in history. Uruguay Round negotiators have agreed to take serious action against discriminatory non-tariff import barriers. And they also have agreed to reduce or eliminate remaining tariffs and quotas on a broad range of products accounting for 85 percent of world trade. These tariffs and quota cuts alone will boost worid output by an estimated $5 trillion in just 10 years. The United States wins in a free-trade regime — and wins big. We already have by far the world's most open market. An agreement binding more than 120 nations to the same discipline can only help our trade balance. The new GATT treaty extends intellectual property protection to U.S. producers who have suffered massive losses from foreign piracy fbr years. It brings service-sector and agricultural exports — two global markets the United States dominates — under a broad range of international fair-trade rules for the first time. It provides a forum for future improvements. American output growth as a direct result of these and other new GATT provisions is estimated at more than $100 billion a year, or more than 20 percent of projected growth worldwide. That means more American jobs in high-wage, export-based industries — 1.4 million of them, in fact — and higher quality, lower priced goods for American consumers. Part of the solution to the problems facing this country hes in creating more jobs and improving the U.S. standifrd of living. Expanded trade does both. These benefits are largely unchallenged by GATT's opponents. General economic "arguments" against free trade collapsed during last year's NAFTA debate, and this year's NAFTA success stories confirm the benefits. Opponents complain about a purported loss of U.S. state and federal "sovereignty" under GAIT. They warn that American laws might be threatened by decisions ofthe GATT-established Worid Ttade Organization (WTO), fbr example. The fact is that WTO cannot change U.S. law; only Congress can do that. The dispute-resolution provisions of GATT that are at issue here exist because the United States, too often frustrated in efforts to open overseas markets, insisted on them. We want stronger rules — we will benefit from them. The WTO was set up to make sure other countries cannot cheat and that they abide by all the rules, not just the ones they choose to recognize. So what's the problem? The problem is that GATT opponents cannot win their case on the merits, so they are urging "delay," knowing full well that delay kills GATT's political chances as well as compromises America's leadership position in the world. The congressional turnover produced by November's election was stunning. Washington was sent a very clear message on Nov. 8: Voters are tired of politics as usual. Americans told Congress it's time to work together and it's time to move ahead. The entire world movement for the implementation of GATT —and expanded free trade — will wait on America's leadership, the same leadership that brought the agreement to a successful conclusion. Delay would be a tragedy. American economic growth, job creation and family prosperity are waiting. Congress must pass GATT. Now. Mr. Junkins is chairman, president and CEO of Tfexas /nstruments Inc. and chairman ofthe Alliance for GATT Now. s �Bill Frenzel As world's largest importer and exporter, let's pass GATT T he last vote ofthe 103rd Congress will be the flrst test of the new congressional leadership and it is already upon us. By Dec. 1, Congress must decide whether to approve or reject the bill to implement the Uruguay Round, the world trade agreement that was concluded last December in Geneva. From the point of view of America's interests, the decision should be easy. The United States is both the world's largest importer and exporter, with an enormous stake in a solid set of international trading rules. This agreement is a good deal for American manufacturers and it is strongly supported by the National Association of Manufacturers. The agreement cuts tariffs on U.S. exports by 43 percent; U.S. tariffs by 34 percent. And those are averages. In a number of sectors, sectors in which the United States has strong, highly competitive industries, tariffs abroad will fall to zero. That's the story for pharmaceuticals, earthmoving equipment, farm equipment, medical equipment and paper. Those changes and others add up to real growth. Because of the difficulty of quantifying many of the benefits of the Uruguay Round, all of the estimates are likely to fall short of the real benefits. But those estimates are impressive, including, among other things, the calculations that by 2004 the Uruguay Round will mean at least $65 billion more in U.S. growth than would otherwise have occurred and an estimated additional 250,000 U.S. jobs. Rejecting the Uruguay Round, on the other hand, would mean the end of U.S. leadership as surely as the fall ofthe Berlin Wall meant the end of the Soviet Empire. The United States led the effort that created the GAIT, the system of world trading rules that has been in effect since 1947. We also led the effort that produced the present agreement. The process has been long and difficult. Formally it took seven years, from 1986 to 1993. Really, it was more like 15, since in one form or another the Uruguay Round negotiations have been going on since the last major GATT negotiations, the Tbkyo Round, ended in 1979. Countries around the world, including France, Belgium, India, South Korea and Japan have experienced riots and deep political turmoil as they attempted to meet U.S. demands in the Uruguay Round. If we now abandon the agreement, they will be cautious, to say the least, about following our lead in the future. Fortunately, if somewhat ironically, the House of Representatives is almost certain to approve the agreement. Last year at this time, the House was deeply divided over NAFTA and seemed poised to kill it. This year, the leaders of both parties in the House, including the outgoing speaker and the speaker designate fbr the 104th Congress, .are using their influence to make sure this agreement is approved. By contrast the Senate seems less certain. There are doubts over whether a vote on the bill should be allowed. Tbchnical budgetary considerations and doubts about the effects on American sovereignty have generated suggestions that the vote should be put off. Each of these doubts paves the road to the same grand mistake, namely, abandoning the Uruguay Round. And each is easily answered. The vote cannot be put off for two reasons. First, the world is expecting a vote and would read delay as defeat. Second, the world wouldrightlyconclude the treaty it had woriced on so intensely could not even get a fair hearing in the United States. The fast-track procedures that have governed trade bills since 1974 stipulate that once a trade-agreement bill is introduced, it cannot be amended. The reason fbr that is to ensure that Congress considers the whole package, voting it up or down. Either amending or deferring violates the fast-track process and kills the treaty. The budget issue should not be an issue at all. Virtually every economist who has looked at the Uruguay Round has concluded that taxes to the US Tbeasury will go up, not down, as a result of this agreement It would be silly to reject the agreement just because some tariffs will be cut FinaUy, the World TY-ade Organization is not a threat to US. sovereignty. Only the US. Congress can change U.S. law. But the issue of sovereignty raises an important question. WiU the United States be better off acting completely alone in a one-on-one rough-and-tumble world? Or will our diplomats and negotiators have better luck working in a stable institutional environment with agreed rules of conduct for trade? Commerce needs rules and stabiUty. The United States is the biggest trading nation in the world, and it needs the GATT. We aU need the Uruguay Round. If Congress fails to pass i t the U.S. economy and U.S. citizens wiU suffer. So will the new Congress. Mr. Frenzel is a former Minnesoto congressman and special advisor to the .National Association of Manufacturers. C The Stupid Party's contractual obligation to duck the real issues N 'ot even a month has passed since the RepubUcan landsUde, and already the Stupid Party is starting to unravel. At the RepubUcan governors' conference last week, the chief executives muttered about what a blunder it would beforthe new majority to get hung up on "cultural issues"—i.e., issues the voters were mainly worried about — and from deep within the RepubUcan belly on Capitol HiU there rumbled similar sounds, aU before the lowing herd ofthe 103rd Congress had even lumbered into oblivion. What this noise of dissonance ought to tell us is what every schoolboy already knew about RepubUcans: They're not a unified party, and since a good amount of ideological unity is essential for successfulrepresentativegovemment, the chance for arealRepubUcan — let alone a serious conservative — revolution are slim. Yet even aside from the Backstabber Faction of the party, which specializes in making sure the party's platform, issues and even some of its official candidates never jeopardize Democratic and liberal hegemony, whatflopsabout as the core of the RepubUcan right is no great shakes Samuel either. Tb understand this, conFrancis sider the famous "Contract with America," of which Republican rightists are busting with pride these days. The Contract is a series of 10 Samuel Francis, a columnist for "pledges" on the issues that Newt The Washington Times, is nationaUy Gingrich and other GOP sagamores syndicated His column appears here beUeved were important for the party to run on this faU and get to TUesday artd Friday. work on as early as possible when they return in the 104th Congress next year. Fbr the most part, the pledges are harmless enough, though sometimes dressed up in bumper-stickerese — like the "American Dream Restoration Act" — that even poUtical cynics find embarrassing. But what is most striking about the Contract is what isn't in it — among other things, the aforementioned "cultural issues," about which Uberal GOP governors are so worried. Since it is precisely these issues that have been at the heart of the conservative critique of the CUnton administration and indeed of the UberaUsm that has dominated this country for 60 years, there's very httle in the Contractforreal rightists to moo about. Thus, the Contract says nothing about abortion or school prayer, the staple issues of thereUgiousright that contributed significantly to the RepubUcan victory. It says nothing about reversing judicial activism or its anti-constitutionaUst roots that brought us those issues. It says nothing about homosexuals in the military or about the matter of "homosexualrights"in general. The Contract says nothing about affirmative action or other civil rights issues, though Thomas EdsaU, writing in The Washington Post last week, noted that the defection of white men fromtiieDemocrats "violates a core concept at the heart of the Democratic Party as the party of working people. White men are those experiencing the largest wage declines, the brunt of defense cutbacks and the dramatic attenuation of corporate loyalty." If Uberal reporters Uke Mr. EdsaU can understand the crisis white males face today and the poUtical debt RepubUcans owe them, why can't RepubUcans? The Contract says nothing about immigration, legal or iUegal; nothing about gun control; nothing about the 10th Amendment; and nothing about aboUshing federal )rograms and agencies that have ong been on the RepubUcan butch- er block — the Education Department, the Energy Department the Occupational Safety and Health not be placed under U.N. command; good enough, but there's nothing about reversing globalism and protecting national sovereignty, let alone trade issues. What the Contract does do is commit the RepubUcans to a number of economic and fiscal poUcies that vow to benefit various GOP constituencies — businessmen, the elderly, the armed forces. Most of this is reasonable from a partisan point of view, but it does nothing to break with or reverse the New Deal model of Big Government, by which the federal leviathan manages social and economic relations for the benefit of specific client interests. Indeed, Franklin Roosevelt is a hero to Mr. Gingrich. The new mjuority may or may not have enough unity to pass the Contract's promises, but even if it does, they're not what serious conservatives wanted, and passing them wouldn't even begin to loosen the iron grip in which a dominant UberaUsm has held this country for so long. (2 �DOUG BANDOW s Wi etching the Clinton idministration attempt to construct a sensible policy fbr Bosnia is like viewing the famed Swedish "art" film called " I am Curious (Yellow)." Thetideofthe administration's Balkans policy should be " I am Curious (Bosnia)." Just what is the administration hoping to achieve with its on-agiin, offagain demand for greater NATO military involvement in the Balkans? Fbr more than three years civil war has raged in the former Yugoslavia, with succeeding rounds of murder and ethnic "cleansing." Fbr the most part the Serbs have triumphed, but at significant cost: Cemeteries are filled with Serbs as well as Croats and Muslims, hundreds of thousands of Serb civil-, ians are refugees, and Serbia is an intemationai pariah with a bankrupt economy. The European Community, the United States, NATO, and United Nations have all unsuccessfully sought to end the fighting. Washington attempted to keep Yugoslavia together; Germany accelerated the country's breakup. The United Nations inserted international troops into Bosnia to keep the warring parties apart; America pushed for NATO military action, turning peacekeeping personnel into potential targets. The West imposed an arms ban against Bosnia, which, unlike the Bosnian Serbs, had no local source of weapons, and economic sanctions against Serbia, after it lost control over the conflict that it had helped set into motion. International negotiators proposed a succession of complex population reshufflings to be enforced by NATO troops. All of these efforts came to naught, as the war alternately sputtered along and Balkans pornography flared anew. After going on a short-lived offensive a few weeks ago, the Muslim govemment of Bosnia is again losing ground, with Bosnian Serbs threatening the Muslim enclave aroynd the town of Bihac. Western nations have responded with a tor_ _ _ _ _ _ _ rent of words — Bihac is a U.N.-proclaimed "safe area" — and a few military pinpricks, such as cratering a Serb airfield. The Serbs barely noticed It is time to disengage, dropping both sanctions against Serbia and the arms embargo against Bosnia and letting the Europeans handle what is J^anceTiIe a European problem.United States, with no forces " " ^ o n the ground, demanded a more severe bombing campaign, which could endanger U.N. peacekeeping forces, so the Europeans demurred. Now Defense Secretary William Perry says additional air strikes would be ineffective. Bihac burns while NATO bickers. But how could it be any other way? The alliance's earlier raids against tents and abandoned gun emplacements were embarrassments, but even a more serious air effort would not likely change the course of the war. And intervention with ground troops would be jumping into a bloody tar pit, with no prospect of "victory." Despite their oft-expressed humanitarian concern, neither Western Europe nor the United States has sufficient interests at stake to warrant taking the painful military steps necessary to defeat the Serbs. Fbr instance, while the fighting in Bosnia is horrifying, it is no worse than that in Afghanistan, Angola, Armenia, Georgia, Liberia, Somalia, Sudan, Tajikistan, and else- where. What makes the former Yugoslavia unique is that white Europeans are killing each other, guaranteeing a level of media attention and popular concern denied to dead Muslims in the Ttanscaucasus and black animists in Africa. The fact that Bosnia is closer to the major industrial powers of Europe has led to numerous strained analogies to World War I , which was triggered by an assassination in Sarajevo. But that conflict resulted because a half- dozen European powers were willing to go to war over what should have remained a local struggle. No nation has made the same mistake today, creating an effective firebreak to the war's spread despite three years of intense fighting. Indeed, even if the United States wanted to try to remate the map of the Balkans, it is unclear what it should do. Although one may rightly ascribe a large share of villainy to the Serbs, the Croats and Mus- lims are hardly innocents. Consider: If Croats havetileright to secede from a Serb-dominated Yiigoslaviaia, then why don't Serbs have the right to secede from Croatia? Bosnia's Muslim govemment says that it wants to maintain a multiethnic republic, but, of course, it did not want to remain in Yugoslavia as part of a larger multi-ethnic republic. There are few self-evident rights or wrongs in the Balkans imbroglio, other than the uniformly ugly resort to force and murder of noncombatants. The coming change in party control of Congress gives the administration a good excuse to remake a failed policy that has drawn us closer to miUtary involvement in a bitter civil war. It is time to disengage, dropping both sanctions against Serbia and the arms embargo against Bosnia—without becoming the latter's armorer — and letting the Europeans handle what is a European problem. Then the American pubUc would no longer have to be curious about a poUcy that is as ineffective as it is incoherent. Doug Bandow, a senior fellow at] the Cato Institute, is a nationally < syndicated columnist ' This federal agency is a model of reinventing govemment I wish to respond to the item "More red tape" in your Oct. 24 "Inside the Beltway" column. John McCaslin's portrayal of the recent realignment of the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration's (NOAA) headquarters missed the mark. Rather than "beefing up" bureaucracy, as the article would lead the reader to beUeve, we have accomplished the opposite. In the fuU spirit of Vice President Al Gore's National Performance Review (NPR), we undertook a thorough reassessment of the NOAA headquarters structure with an eye toward streamlining functions and eliminating unnecessary layers of bureaucracy. A simple count of the number of offices before and after the reaUgnment ignores the fact that by abolishing the Offlce of External Affairs in NOAA, we eUminated an entire layer of bureaucracy that was overseeing the work of NOAA's headquarters staff offices. We created a flatter organizational structure with a broader span of supervisory control consistent with NPR recommendations. In the process, we were able to reduce the overaU staffing level at NOAA headquarters by 12 percent. Moreover, we have been able to empower the staff offices and individual employees at headquarters by providing them with a clearer sense of their roles and responsibiUties. NOAA is committed to reinventing govemment and improving our performance and service to the American pubUc. As an example, NOAA volunteered to serve as a pUot for implementation of the Govemment Performance and Results Act and was selected as one of 10 exemplary federal agencies. I beUeve it does a disservice to our employees to ridicule the hard wortc and efforts that are under way to improve government and eliminate red tape. D. JAMES BAKER Undersecretary for Oceans and Atmosphere US. FRED SINGER Climate claims wither under luminous lights of science lhe Global Climate lY-eaty and all activities to limit the emission of greenhouse gases are driven by hyped fears of a global warming catastrophe that's based on climate model calculations, rather than on actual climate data. By now, thanks to generous spending of taxpayers' fimds, a widespread constituency has been created in support of policies to overcome this phantom threat, consisting mostly of international conferenceers: U.N. bureaucrats, assorted politicians, environmental activists, and some compliant scientists. (By the way, the research budget for "global change" is now at the $1.8 biUion level in the United States; the global total is of course much higher, particularly when nonresearch activities are added.) But just how trustworthy are the calculations of a major global warming? A 1990 Policymakers Summary by the U.N.-sponsored Intergovernmental Panel on CUmate Change (IPCC) assures us that the global temperature record of the past 100 years is "broadly consistent with predictions of cUmate models." Nothing could be furtherfromthe truth! With the pubUc becoming less concerned about greenhouse warming, partly because of the cooUng effect of the volcano Pinatubo, the IPCC felt compeUed to stir up fears by issuing a press release on SepL 15, reiterating scare forecasts and also claiming to have found "greater confidence in the computer models." But the underlying, just completed IPCCreportdoes not support this claim, which is pure invention. To add embarrassment: A news account in a recent issue of the journal Science revealed that modelers have to "cheat a Uttle" and fudge their numbers to get the models to reproduce the correct current cUmate, throwing even more doubts of forecasts. Nevertheless, the IPCC claim of "broadly consistent" has become the mantra of activists pushing to control the emission of carbon dioxide by curtailing energy use. This artfuUy worded phrase is really a cop-out, covering up the fact that the models have faUed miserably in accounting for past temperature changes, cannot explain the highly accurate global record from weather sateUites —and are internally inconsistent to boot. Model predictions of future global warming vary by 300 percent — from 1.5 degrees to 4.5 degrees Centigrade, for a doubled atmospheric concentration of greenhouse gases—and even by more on a regional scale. B«evlef.1993. p.340. ; a n lo i n The Washington Times TUESDAY, NOVEMBER 29,1994 '5 �T PHILIP TERZIAN O ne of the more amusing dividends of Republican control of Congress has been the horror with which Jesse Helms is anticipated as chairman of the Senate Foreign Relations Committee. During a brief visit to New England recently, I was asked on a half-dozen occasions if (in so many words) the Republic could endure two years of Chairman Jesse. It will endure, I replied; it always has. Of course, in my view, anyone who causes such special discomfort must be doing something right. After 40-plus years on the Democ, ratic plantation, "responsible" Republicans on Capitol Hill have fallen into the age-old subservient habit of currying favor by tugging theirforelock,sincerely apologizing . to their masters for dissent, and thankfully scraping up those legislative crumbs tossed in their direction every now and then. Jesse Helms does not fit this description. He has no interest in . accumulating brownie points with people who dislike him — or even with those who generally agree with him. He is wholly indifferent to Newsweek's approval, or the blessing of the Council on Fbreign Relations, or the American Enterprise Institute, or any university which has not yet awarded him an honorary degree. One of the dangers of public life in the nation's capital is the tendency of people such as Mr. Helms to go native: l b worry, in time, whether the permanent managerial class (the press, the think tanks, the Democratic hostesses) will invite them to dinner, or David S. Broder will praise them as moderates. Jesse Helms is completely unconcerned with such matters. Which is good. Part of what divides him from the ordinary run of politicians is his deliberate indifference to fashion and convention. This is not to say that he is without scruples, or principles, or judgment about things; but it is to say that his judgment, principles and scruples are measured against self-imposed, not external, standards. I f Mr. Helms were anything other than a Reading the policy compass under Potential bonus right-wing RepubUcan, this would earn him considerable stature as one of the Senate's historic mavericks: One with John C. Calhoun or (in our time) the La FbUettes, father and son, or Wayne Morse. Thke, for example, his offhand assertion this past weekend that President CUnton "is not up" to the job of commander in chief — a view, he claims, that is shared by the bulk of the nation's armed services. Whether or not that latter aUegadon is true, I don't know; but the evidence exists. So does the evidence for Mr. Hehns' basic theorem. It is not an outlandish opinion, and in fact is shared by many Democrats in Washington: The only thing outlandish is its pubUc declaration. And only Mr. Helms, among senators at any rate, would say it. As always, the responses are better theater than the provocation. Gen. John Shalikashvili, chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, waited 24 hours before pubUcly denying that people in the militaiy beUeve Mr. CUnton is not their ideal commander in chief. MeanwhUe, Sen. Robert Dole, who would Uke to be president, aUowed that Mr. Ointon has "grown" into the job. Look at Haiti, he added —where Raoul Cedras's 800-man vaudeviUe army was disarmed and dispersed by soldiers of the 10th Mountain Infantry Division. Better yet, Mr. Helms' comments gave such unlikely defenders of presidential dignity as Sens. Tbm Harkin of Iowa and Chris Dodd of Connecticut a chance to sound for once like Col. Blimp: They were shocked to hear such contemptuous remarks about the president — forgetting, perhaps, their own disdainful rhetoric about earlier IN TVEtfCfc C T J T A P F WH S N CHIEF, I H k O D R D A W REE SR IA U G L STRIKE- A C T NO T D Y ON O A ! (unfortunately RepubUcan) commanders in chief.
